#2947. Fantasy Football Start 'Em Sit 'Em: Justin Boone's Week 9 lineup advice

Justin Boone provides a weekly look at undervalued fantasy players to consider starting this week and potential busts you might want to leave on your bench.

For the rest of your lineup decisions, consult his Week 9 rankings.

It's almost surprising that Prescott is the QB11 in fantasy points per game this year, considering he went a month without a multi-touchdown game at one point. Fortunately, he's found the end zone at least twice in each of the past two weeks.

Now, Prescott faces a Colts team that's circling the drain before a likely selloff at the trade deadline. In its first game after dealing Quenton Nelson and DeForest Buckner, Indy was dismantled 42-7 by the Jaguars. The Cowboys are 5.5-point favorites on the road with the fourth-highest implied point total on the week.

Though Dak hasn't been the most reliable fantasy option this season, you can start him confidently as a top-five QB versus the Colts.

Malik Willis vs. Lions – With the Dolphins' young receiving corps getting healthy, Willis has been heating up lately as a solid fantasy streamer who offers a high floor and high ceiling. This is setting up to be a potential ceiling game for him, against a Detroit defense giving up the most fantasy points to QBs, including multiple TDs in every outing.

C.J. Stroud at Chargers – Stroud has quietly had a very solid season and, with the Texans' struggles in the run game, you can expect the team to continue relying on his arm. The 25-year-old is tied for the fifth most fantasy points among quarterbacks over the last five weeks. Coming off the bye, Stroud should get a boost from Marvin Harrison Jr. getting more comfortable in the offense and Tank Dell seeing a rise in snaps.

Brown only finished as the RB20 in a great matchup with the Titans last week, but that would have been a top-12 fantasy back if his 20-yard touchdown catch wasn't nullified by a penalty.

The good news is that Brown continues to dominate the Bengals backfield, playing almost 70% of the snaps last week and turning them into 20 touches, 101 scrimmage yards and eight catches.

He also gets another positive matchup with the Falcons defense that's the fourth-easiest matchup for fantasy running backs. Atlanta has allowed seven touchdowns to RBs over its last three games and just got decimated for over 300 rushing yards by the Buccaneers backs in Week 8.

I know it's been frustrating at times having Brown in your lineup; just don't give up now. Bigger days are within reach. If you are planning to sell him, you'll want to wait until after this game to get the most value.

Jonah Coleman at Panthers – Coleman returned from IR and immediately took control of the Broncos backfield, playing 58% of the snaps over the last two weeks and racking up 173 scrimmage yards, six receptions and a touchdown. Now, he gets a Panthers defense that's permitting the second-most fantasy points to his position. The rookie is a top-15 fantasy RB option this week.

Blake Corum at Commanders – Over the last month, the Rams offense has leaned on their backfield more with Davante Adams hitting IR. That resulted in both backs averaging top-15 fantasy stats, with Corum at RB13 and Kyren Williams at RB15. Don't be misled; Williams is still the lead back and Corum hasn't played over 50% of the snaps in any game. However, he's getting plenty of opportunity, which should continue as 5.5-point favorites over the Commanders.

Tyler Allgeier at Seahawks – While I hate the matchup, it's impossible to argue with the setup for Allgeier. Jeremiyah Love is on IR and Bam Knight is in concussion protocol. That leaves Allgeier as the last man standing in Arizona's RB room. Though you can't expect another 191-yard, five-catch, two-touchdown performance (because he's not playing the Cowboys defense), Allgeier can still be started as a volume-based, low-end RB2.

Jefferson isn't a name that makes it to the Start section very often because he's such a talented player that fantasy managers don't need a reminder. However, given the state of the quarterback play in Minnesota, starting Jefferson wasn't as obvious as it had been in the past.

The veteran wideout has been held to receiving-yard totals of 55, 32, 44 and 33 in four of his last five outings and Kyler Murray wasn't finding his rhythm in Kevin O'Connell's scheme.

Murray's new hamstring injury gave the coaching staff a chance to make a change and they jumped on it, naming Carson Wentz the starter for Monday's matchup with the Bills. With Buffalo's defense still being a work in progress, Wentz and company should be able to get the Vikings' passing game back on track.

That's great news for Jefferson, who has averaged 6.4 receptions and 89.1 receiving yards during the seven games where Wentz has played at least 80% of the snaps over the last two seasons. That includes an 8-92-2 stat line from the opener this year.

This move pushed Jefferson back into the top-10 fantasy WRs and in the conversation for a spot in the top-five for as long as Wentz is under center.

George Pickens at Colts – Pickens has been playing through shoulder and hamstring injuries the last couple of weeks and his playing time has taken a slight hit because of it. Jerry Jones was very clear this week that he believes Pickens needs to be getting the ball more, so this could be the rare squeaky wheel game initiated by the owner. As it stands, Pickens is still 14th in receiving yards among wideouts, but that ranking could rise thanks to a matchup with the Colts.

Rashee Rice vs. Jets – For all the concerns about Rice before the season, he's actually performed well in every outing. Rice has at least 83 yards or a touchdown in all four of his healthy appearances, including a 7-53-1 stat line in his first game back last week, despite only playing 56% of the snaps. That number should rise in a favorable spot against a Jets defense that plays a lot of single-high coverage, which Rice excels against.

Rome Odunze vs. Buccaneers – Odunze is 20th in receiving yards, but 53rd in fantasy points scored among wideouts. The discrepancy exists due to a lack of touchdowns, with Odunze not finding the end zone yet this season. Fortunately, the offense has been regaining its form with Caleb Williams back and Odunze has posted two of his best outings this season in recent weeks, with 69 yards against the Patriots and 87 yards versus the Seahawks. Next up is a Bucs defense that's allowed five different WRs to top 85 yards in the last month.

Through the first five weeks, LaPorta was a top-five fantasy tight end against one of the easier schedules at his position. Then came a bye, followed by back-to-back matchups against the two toughest defenses against his position: the Packers and Vikings.

That led conspiracy theorists to speculate that the team was punishing LaPorta for his comments about being frustrated with contract extension talks with the organization. I'm guessing the degree of difficulty being turned up on him had more to do with it.

Thankfully, a softer matchup has arrived with the Dolphins defense giving up the third-most fantasy points to tight ends. The Lions are also tied for the second-highest implied point total on the slate. This all points to a bounce-back week for LaPorta, which is why he's back in my top five.

Kyle Pitts Sr. vs. Bengals – After three straight top-12 TE finishes, Pitts finally came back down to earth with just two catches for six yards in a rainy loss to the Bucs last week. Now, he suits up for a potential shoutout in the dome against a Bengals defense that's the ninth-most generous unit to tight ends.

Mike Gesicki at Falcons – You can also start the other tight end in that game, with Gesicki having a great chance to extend his touchdown streak versus Atlanta. The veteran has scored a touchdown in six of his seven outings and currently sits as the TE6 in fantasy points per game. You could do worse than streaming him in a game that's tied for the highest projected point total at 52.5.

It seems like the distant past now, but Young was the QB4 overall in fantasy points per game through the first six weeks, in part thanks to an easy early-season schedule. Life comes at you fast, though and a slew of injuries on the offensive line have completely changed the outlook for this offense. It doesn't help that their last two opponents have been much tougher, the Buccaneers and Packers, leading to a test that Young ultimately failed.

Coming off his two lowest fantasy outputs of the season, Young now has another difficult challenge ahead with the Broncos defense that got back Patrick Surtain II last week. There will be better days ahead, including the Saints matchup in Week 10, but for now, Young needs to head to your bench.

Geno Smith at Chiefs – Kansas City's defense is allowing the sixth-fewest fantasy points to quarterbacks and tends to be even more stout at home. Meanwhile, the Jets will be without their top receiver Garrett Wilson, who is in concussion protocol. This isn't the spot to play Smith.

Baker Mayfield at Bears – At some point, Mayfield will begin throwing again and this could be the week, since he's facing a high-scoring Bears offense. But forgive me if I'm not interested in starting a quarterback who hasn't topped 13 fantasy points in a game this season and has only attempted 14 passes over the last two weeks.

White came through in Week 8 thanks to a touchdown, but his usage (13 touches, 47% snap share) was disappointing given the matchup with the Eagles and the fact that Jacory Croskey-Merritt is on IR.

The situation isn't getting better this week against a Rams defense that's his toughest opponent yet. Not only are they allowing the seventh-fewest fantasy points to running backs, but it's possible Myles Garrett could be activated for the game.

This all points to a risky setup for White, who will likely need another touchdown to save his fantasy day again.

Zach Charbonnet vs. Cardinals – The Seahawks are 8.5-point favorites, so the possibility of a goal-line carry is always there. However, it's tough to get overly excited about Charbonnet, who managed just 29 yards on nine touches while playing 37% of the snaps in his season debut. Rookie Jadarian Price displayed far more juice during the game and should earn more touches moving forward, while Charbonnet remains in a supporting role.

Omarion Hampton vs. Texans – Coming off the bye, Hampton played a season-low 29.5% of the snaps. The sophomore back has only topped 56 scrimmage yards once this season. Like most of the running backs in this section, he's purely a TD-or-bust RB3.

Jordan Mason vs. Bills – Mason returned to the lineup in Week 7 with a 64% snap share and 15 carries for 68 yards. Unfortunately, just one week later, those numbers dropped to 28% of the snaps and just eight carries for 20 yards. While the matchup with the Bills is a positive one, it's hard to trust Mason until we see more from him and the Vikings offense as a whole.

Wilson finally rediscovered his ceiling with an eight-catch, 111-yard, one-touchdown performance, but it took a very friendly matchup with the Cowboys to get there.

Wilson also accomplished the feat while playing through two broken ribs, something that will continue to be a challenge this week.

It's also worth remembering that Wilson was held to 55 yards or less in three straight games before that springboard encounter with Dallas. The Seahawks defense, which is giving up the fewest fantasy points to wideouts, won't be the same kind of pushover. That makes Wilson a volatile WR3.

Jalen Coker vs. Broncos – The issues on the offensive line and the difficult matchup with the Broncos defense are more than enough reason to avoid Coker this week. He's only managed three catches over his last two outings.

Chris Godwin Jr. at Bears – Godwin hasn't topped 50 receiving yards in a game this season and hasn't reached 40 yards in his last six appearances. Injuries have simply sapped his effectiveness, and given his age, it's likely his best days are behind him.

Xavier Worthy vs. Jets – Worthy has been held to 25 yards or less in five of his seven games this season. He's also only found the end zone once all year, making him a very shaky flex option at best.

Kincaid's best performances have coincided with DJ Moore being in the lineup, which gives you hope that he can get back to being a top-10 fantasy tight end the rest of the way. Unfortunately, this week's matchup is with the Vikings defense, which is the biggest threat to tight ends by far this season.

Minnesota is allowing just 5.5 fantasy points per game, with the second-place Packers coming in at 7.0 fppg. The Vikings also haven't allowed a touchdown to a tight end and we've yet to see them give up more than 65 yards to a player at the position. Even if you look back to last year, Brian Flores' crew was the eighth-worst opponent for tight ends. If Kincaid comes through this week, it will be an outlier based on what we've seen from this defense in 2026.

Chig Okonkwo vs. Rams – After back-to-back 60-plus-yard games, Okonkwo managed just two catches for 32 yards in a tougher matchup with the Eagles last week. The Rams are the seventh most difficult opponent for fantasy tight ends, so I wouldn't get my hopes up here.

Tyler Higbee at Commanders – Higbee has been held to 22 yards, 14 yards and 14 yards over his last three outings, and it's possible Terrance Ferguson could return for this contest.

#2946. Investors brace for lower Anthropic valuation, calling $1.5 trillion a 'stretch'

Following tech's selloff on Wall Street this week, some investors are lowering their expectations for Anthropic.

The artificial intelligence company had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation in its IPO up until recently, but stakeholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

The change in sentiment comes alongside growing concerns about the outsized spending by AI companies and a sharp post-midterm slump in the market. The Nasdaq plummeted more than 5% on Thursday, its steepest drop in 19 months, reflecting fears that Democrats' newfound power in Washington, D.C., and a nationwide rebuke of AI will lead to a slowdown in the buildout of data centers.

Bearishness on the AI trade was on the rise before the election. The Nasdaq suffered its worst month in October since March 2025, led by declines in chipmakers and neoclouds.

Anthropic is set to list on the Nasdaq, under the ticker ANT, and is gearing up for an expected debut in a matter of weeks. Last month, Anthropic was reportedly seeking a valuation as high as $2 trillion. As of May, the company was privately valued at $965 billion.

With the latest market turmoil, one investor said $1 trillion to $1.25 trillion is a more likely valuation target, although that could change with market conditions. The investors asked not to be named because they're not authorized to speak about the matter.

Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC that, "Investors will expect the IPO to be priced at least at the low end of previous expectations."

"The market really needs this IPO to go well and the bankers on the deal surely understand that," Luria said. "The best way to set Anthropic up for success is to price the deal well."

The company is expected to kick off its roadshow next week.

#2945. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2944. Rapid Recap: Raptors 121, Bucks 107

In a really disappointing opening NBA Cup game, the Milwaukee Bucks lost to the injury-ravaged Toronto Raptors 121-107. The only real positive to come out of the night was Brayden Burries' breakout game (as I predicted!): he scored a career-high 21 points on 8/14 shooting, plus 11 rebounds (five offensive) and six assists. For the Raptors, second-year man Alijah Martin went off for a career-high 27 points on 6/7 from deep.

The Bucks got off to a blinding start, leading 15-4 after five minutes largely thanks to Brayden Burries, who was electric as a rebounder to kick this one off; Burries had a maniacal five rebounds in the first four minutes, two of which came on the offensive glass. Unfortunately, Myles Turner picked up two quick fouls, which meant early minutes for Kel'el Ware; and while Ware did some good things, the Raptors seemed perfectly content leaving him open from three, and the Bucks' offence stalled when he kept missing. Before long, it was a one-possession game after Alijah Martin nailed three spot-up triples in short order. Thankfully, Ryan Rollins scored five points in the final two minutes to put the Bucks up 29-24 after one.

Burries opened the second with a baseline hammer, followed by a Rollins and-one to push Milwaukee's lead out to eight. The Bucks knuckled down on defence for the next few minutes, and a Burries mid-range jumper forced the Raptors to call a timeout as the home side led by double digits about halfway through the period. After going scoreless in the first quarter for the third straight game, Tyler Herro finally began to heat up in the second, nailing a step-back jumper against the waning shot clock and making some great interior passes. Burries was fantastic, nailing his first three to reach a career-high 15 points (on 7/10 shooting) with just over two minutes left in the first half! Much to Milwaukee's dismay, though, Toronto closed on a 6-0 run to cut the deficit to single digits, 60-51, at the major break.

The Bucks began the third lethargic, unable to generate good shots as the Raptors opened on an 11-4 run. Milwaukee was getting by on ticky-tack foul calls, while Toronto was running smooth offence. The visitors took their first lead at the six-minute mark after another Martin triple, but Herro and Jaquez responded with threes of their own to take it back; a transition lob to Nate Ament a few plays later capped Milwaukee's 10-2 run and forced the Raptors timeout. Of course, the visitors responded in a flash with a 6-0 spurt as Jenkins called a timeout. Both teams mostly held serve from there on out, with a late AJ Green bomb bringing the Bucks to within two, 89-87, after three quarters.

The Bucks began the fourth like the third: unfocused and lethargic. With Herro taking a break, Rollins assumed point guard duties and did not do a good job, as he threw a reckless whip pass to Turner in the middle of the floor; the Raptors then went down the other end to grab their own rebound and find Martin for his fifth three of the night to add to his career high—really disappointing stuff. Jenkins had to sub Herro back in much earlier than (I assume) he wanted to, as the Bucks went down seven, but thankfully, AJ Green kept them in it with timely threes. Milwaukee made a few runs but could not execute (on either end) when they needed to, losing in ugly fashion to a depleted Raptors outfit.

Before AJ Green's three at the buzzer, the Bucks had scored just 17 points in the fourth. The physical Raptors defence had completely strangled them.

Myles Turner would beat out pillows, butter, and kittens for the softest things on this earth.

#2943. The Daily: Why Red Wings’ Next Four Games Are Critical; Augustine Delivering in GR

The Detroit Red Wings (7-7-3) start a four-game road trip tonight against Colorado Avalanche and it feels these games represent a pivotal stretch for team.

"We can't shoot ourselves in the foot in these games if we put ourselves in a good position to win," Red Wings coach Todd McLellan said. "We've got to find ways to close it out, which we've done in Florida, done in Edmonton against some really good teams."

But the truth is every game is critical for the Red Wings as they continue to establish a new identity without Dylan Larkin, with younger player taking on key roles, with uncertainty surrounding Simon Edvinsson and with Shawn Horcoff in command as interim general manager.

The Red Wings have been competitive in the early going, but with three losses in the last four games, they don't want any of last season's bad habits creeping into their game. If they can get through these net four games with modest success, they will be in a position to take advantage of a more favorable schedule. They will only play five more road games among the next 16 games before Christmas.

The goal is to enter that stretch feeling good about how they are playing.

"We have to get off to good starts," McLellan said. "We need some production again from key people. When they get their looks and touches they are going to have to produce. Our penalty kill has been improving. It's going to have to stay at that level and our power play is going to have to get us something."

#2942. Tech companies face mounting child safety lawsuits while pushing AI into schools

Tech companies have spent years telling parents they're working to make their products safer for children. But ongoing jury trials, settlements and related litigation against the likes of Meta, Google and Snap suggest that they're not doing enough.

The latest examples landed this week. Snap confidentially settled a case claiming that its technology caused severe psychological distress to two teenage users. Meta, TikTok and YouTube previously settled with the plaintiffs in the same suit. And Google was sued this week by the parents of a child who allegedly suffered a psychological crisis after chatting with the Gemini AI chatbot in Google Classroom.

Concern is intensifying as artificial intelligence companies pursue trillion-dollar valuations, and established internet giants pour money into developing advanced AI models while pushing them into the classroom. Products and features intended to maximize engagement can have detrimental effects on users, thus warranting "broader systemic changes in tech design, particularly when children are using them," said developmental psychologist Kate Blocker.

"I think it's so so important that children's safety is being considered when these products — AI, social media, et cetera — are being designed, and not after the fact as these problems emerge," said Blocker, the director of research and programs for the nonprofit Children and Screens.

Snap's settlement this week, which spared CEO Evan Spiegel from testifying in a Los Angeles court, continues a general trend of tech companies attempting to shield themselves from public scrutiny due to the various high-profile child-safety related trials this year, said Joseph McNally, the director of emerging litigation at McNicholas & McNicholas.

The case was one of several lawsuits related to the social media personal injury trial that Meta and YouTube lost earlier this year. More trials are slated to commence in March.

"I view this settlement and the other settlements in this space as an effort by tech companies to turn a corner, not only because I think that there were real legal challenges, but also the reputation of their products, the reputation of their companies has really been hit," said McNally, a former acting U.S. attorney for the Central District of California.

"The parties are pleased to have been able to resolve this matter in an amicable manner," a Snap spokesperson said in a statement.

But there remains a federal case involving Meta, TikTok, Snap and YouTube and a litany of nationwide school districts, along with various state attorneys general suits. In August, Meta agreed to pay nearly $17 billion to settle with a group of state AGs in a child mental health case. Some AGs are still pursuing similar suits against Meta's rivals.

The social media lawsuits have helped inform parents and the public about how the industry designs products used by teenagers and children, McNally said.

"It's the technology arms race that continues," he said. "What is oftentimes missing is how you balance that participation in the arms race with utility and then product safety."

Google ran into more criticism this week for moving too fast. The New York Times reported on Tuesday that K-12 schools that don't allow use of Google's AI tools for students will lose access to search. Chris Phillips, vice president for education at Google, told the Times that, "Search and AI are deeply integrated."

That coincided with the new lawsuit against Google centering on a teen's alleged suicidal crisis that the parents attributed to the company's Gemini AI. The plaintiff's attorneys allege that "despite Gemini's known risks, Google hastily integrated" Gemini into Chromebook computers for school children.

"We are reviewing the claims in the lawsuit and we take this very seriously," a Google spokesperson said in a statement. "We've worked extensively with medical professionals and academic experts to build features that help young people find critical support and trusted resources when they need it."

Google and its internet peers have for decades been able to rely on Section 230.

Passed in 1996, the federal legislation has protected social media companies from being sued over content posted by their users. That's why plaintiffs' attorneys and state AGs have been focused on alleged harms stemming from the design of the tech platforms as opposed to their content.

Some plaintiffs are using the same tactics against AI companies. OpenAI faces several lawsuits from state AGs and plaintiff attorneys, taking cues from the social media suits in alleging that the design of ChatGPT can pose risks to children.

The litigation could become a growing financial risk as the company plans for a potential blockbuster IPO. OpenAI competitor Anthropic, which is planning to go public this month, highlighted the issue in its prospectus, even though the Claude creator prohibits children from using its product.

"This is a clear indication that tech companies anticipate more litigation risks than they did a decade ago," said Michele Neitz, a professor at the University of San Francisco School of Law.

Blocker said that the highly personalized nature of current AI-powered chat apps makes it "a little bit unpredictable" to determine exactly what the apps will say to kids. She stressed the importance of testing and evaluating these products for children's developmental safety from the start, "not after harms start happening."

This collective litigation has helped educate the public on the potential pitfalls of poor product design choices, Blocker said. And it could be a catalyzing force for change in the tech industry.

"Certainly there's a hope that this will motivate real systemic change, both from a regulation standpoint and internal company decision-making standpoint," Blocker said.

If you are having suicidal thoughts or are in distress, contact the Suicide & Crisis Lifeline at 988 for support and assistance from a trained counselor.

#2941. Is Brandon Aiyuk returning to 49ers? Latest on NFL's greatest soap opera

The saga between the San Francisco 49ers and wide receiver Brandon Aiyuk has been playing out for more than a year. The last 24 hours has been something out of a soap opera.

The latest turn saw Aiyuk show up to the 49ers practice facility with his recently hired agent, Drew Rosenhaus, for a meeting with the Niners' front office at Levi's Stadium in Santa Clara, California, on Friday, Nov. 6.

Aiyuk brought Rosenhaus into the fold in an attempt to be reinstated to the NFL for a potential comeback. Both addressed reporters before meeting with the Niners' front office.

"Obviously Brandon is here to meet with the team. Fortunately, he's feeling good physically. He's healthy. He's ready to get back to work," Rosenhaus told reporters. "Don't really have any comment about where he's going to play. We'll keep that between us and the Niners, but obviously we're here with all the right intentions."

Aiyuk hasn't played since Week 7 of the 2024 season, when he was sidelined with an MCL and ACL injury that required surgery. He missed rehab sessions and the entire 2025 season. A year later, he says he's fully healthy and "100%" ready to get back to playing football.

"I'm feeling great, feeling healthy in my recovery process, in my rehab process," Aiyuk said. "(I'm) back to 100% and looking to get reinstated into the National Football League."

When asked if he's hoping to play for the Niners again, Aiyuk responded by saying that his hope is "to get reinstated into the National Football League."

The scheduled meeting between Aiyuk and the Niners happened, but not much information came out of it.

After the meeting, Rosenhaus was even more brief than he was walking into it.

"To be continued," Rosenhaus said. "We don't have any further statements right now. But when we do we'll let you know."

As far as who was a part of the meeting with Aiyuk and Rosenhaus, Aiyuk said "we met with the bosses, man. Big bosses."

At this point it appears the relationship he had with the team, head coach Kyle Shanahan and teammates has seemingly dissolved. After practice, Shanahan seemed less than enthused to discuss Aiyuk after saying he has no role in conversations surrounding the wide receiver.

"No clue. Your guess is as good as mine," Shanahan said about what's going to happen with Aiyuk. He added that he could "care less" about what happens.

"It's been over for our football team for a long time, so I don't hear much except questions about him today. But, it's not too big of a deal for me to answer," Shanahan said.

Multiple players, including Fred Warner, Christian McCaffrey and George Kittle, didn't comment in detail on Aiyuk's situation.

"It's not my place, really," 49ers defensive lineman Nick Bosa said. "I'll dap him up. No hard feelings between me and him."

Shanahan told reporters that he sees the Aiyuk situation as "zero to do with (the) football team".

"Anything that has zero to do with our football team, especially during the season, I will give zero thoughts to," Shanahan said.

Before Aiyuk can return to the 49ers, he may have to pay his dues − literally. According to reports, the wide receiver owes San Francisco nearly $13 million in bonus forfeiture.

Aiyuk was the 25th overall selection in the 2020 NFL Draft out of Arizona State. He's spent his whole career with San Francisco, where he's tallied 294 catches, 4,305 yards and 25 touchdowns in 69 games with the Niners.

Aiyuk was born in Rocklin, California, about 20 miles outside of Sacramento. The 28-year-old was raised in Reno, Nevada, where he starred at Robert McQueen High School. He returned to Rocklin to play football at Sierra College, a junior college, before transferring to Arizona State.

After trailing by as many as 16 points in the first half, Philadelphia fought back to pull within just two points midway through the third quarter. This time, however, the Cavaliers absorbed Philly's counterpunch and put the Sixers back on the ropes. Philadelphia falls to 1-1 in NBA Cup East Group B action, blowing up their point differential in the process. Let's take a look at tonight's positive performances before moving past this one.

VJ Edgecombe: 19 points, 3 rebounds, 2 assists, 4 steals, 0 turnovers

#2939. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2938. Why a wave of bomb threats is proving so difficult for Europe's aviation authorities to contain

A spate of bomb threats against passenger flights has forced diversions, emergency landings and military escorts across European airspace this week, stoking fear among passengers and heaping pressure on airlines.

Authorities have described the more than two dozen threats as malicious hoaxes, and no actual attacks have been carried out. But the incidents have highlighted the huge impact that even false claims can have on a tightly scheduled aviation network.

"This is really quite exceptional, I've never seen this before in my whole career," Wilfried Covent, head of security firm Covent Consulting with a specialization in aviation, told CNBC by phone.

"We've had bomb threats in flights and towards airports, but usually maybe a single copycat, one or two max. What we see now is a systematic attack on airlines and airports."

An email sent to European broadcasters on Friday claimed to be from the leader of the group behind the plane hoaxes and also said bombs had been placed in their buildings, prompting evacuations. No bombs were found at the sites.

"What happened this week is just a small part of what awaits Europe in the near future," the message said, according to Reuters.

The news agency said it could not verify the sender's identity, where the emails originated or whether they were linked to the threats against flights.

Covent said the "criminals" — who many security analysts say are likely to be state-backed actors working in coordination to disrupt public life for Europeans, but have not been identified — were well aware that the "weakest point of aviation is targeting it in-flight."

"With a threat on an airport or a general threat to aircraft, you can assess the situation without fully disrupting operations. When the threat is called on a specific flight number that is in the air, even with a hoax, that triggers international regulations which ensure you don't ignore it — it means a quick landing, evacuation and searches by specialist forces," he told CNBC.

"For the context of an assessment, you cannot say that 20 times before it has been a hoax so this time it is a hoax, the safety of passengers and crew is priority number one. The criminals know that."

Responsibility for dealing with hoax attacks is split, international agencies told CNBC. Assessing security threats and implementing aviation security measures primarily falls to national authorities, working in coordination with airport operators, airlines and law enforcement.

Air traffic control will also be involved in finding aircraft with an appropriate place to land, while the European Union Aviation Safety Agency, or EASA and U.K.'s Civil Aviation Authority more broadly set aviation safety rules and set how airlines prepare their crews to respond to security threats. Within the European Union, the European Commission is involved in aviation security policy and coordination between member states.

The operational decisions also depend on where the threat arises. Joji Waites, director of flight safety, policy and regulation at the British Air Line Pilots Association, said airline assessors grade a threat as red, amber or green, but that an amber verdict would never be given if the plane is airborne.

If assessors judge a threat credible, the crew is informed and the captain takes command of decisions for the aircraft, guided by company procedures. On the ground, that may mean an evacuation, while in the air the priority is to land as soon and as safely as possible.

As well as the fear and disruption to specific flights, each diversion can then affect the next flight scheduled to use that aircraft, the crew's working hours and passengers with connections.

The network effect can be significant. Philip Baum, a visiting professor of aviation security at Coventry University, told AP that a single diversion could cost an airline as much as $250,000, including replacement crews, missed connections and disruption to the rest of the day's schedule.

"Diversions necessitated by these bomb threats are a disturbance to a system designed for predictability and efficiency, and lead to increased workload for pilots, Air Traffic Controllers and airports," an EASA spokesperson said in emailed comments.

"As these staff have all have been trained to cope with such unexpected developments, the immediate safety threat from individual hoax threats is low. However, the higher number of diversions places an additional strain on the system."

National governments will already be looking at their aviation authority regulations and frameworks and there will be discussions at the European and global level, John Edwards, senior consultant at Gates Aviation in London, said by phone.

"Bomb threats to aviation have been going on for decades, what they'll need to be looking at is what's different. Why have there been so many, why have so many been considered sufficiently credible to warrant the kind of action that it's warranted?"

"There's a range of questions they should be able to work through fairly quickly to establish a broad-brush level of credibility. If there is some characteristic that is new, every time there is an event of this kind, it triggers a big collaboration to ensure recurrence is prevented going forward."

"In theory, he said, "the vast majority should be able to assessed and considered non-credible based on routine risk assessment processes and well established procedures."

However, Eric Schouten, a former intelligence liaison officer at the Dutch General Intelligence and Security Service and CEO of security consultancy Dyami, said that changes to existing procedural regulations would take some time and require balancing the views and responsibilities of countries, airports, safety authorities and the European Union, with geopolitics also playing a role.

"There is a high likelihood even more flights are going to be canceled or diverted. It's possible that next to be targeted could be railways and highways," he said.

"But they will figure it out. It's not the first time airlines have had to deal with hoax calls because there are always copycats."

"Whatever measures they come out with to address it, they won't share publicly" for risk of alerting the bad actors, he added.

Wilfried Covent said that while there would not be any quick changes to assessment protocol, "if this is really going on for ever and ever something will need to change or you'd have to stop flying completely."

"Based on what we've seen in the past with other kinds of threats, time goes on and the type of attack may change and move on," he continued, pointing to the wave of cyberattacks that has recently hit supermarket fridges and blood banks.

#2937. 'I Don't Look At It As Helping My Game': Why Maple Leafs' John Tavares Is Slated To Return As Winger In Likely Return Against Canadie

John Tavares is expected to return to the Toronto Maple Leafs lineup after roughly three weeks out, and the early indication is that he will do so on the wing.

Tavares skated on the second line of the wing alongside Nick Paul and William Nylander. He said the process has been challenging but positive, giving credit to the medical and performance staff for getting him back.

"Feeling good," Tavares said. "It's been a good three weeks now, I guess. And excited to be right there. ... Just excited, love to compete, and want to help the team."

The shift to the wing is not entirely new territory. Tavares played left wing next to Paul in the final game before he was injured. Coach Jim Hiller had spoken with him about the possibility in the offseason, and Tavares had already logged time on the wing for Canada at the World Championship in May. While he has spent the bulk of his Leafs career down the middle, he said the team's depth makes flexibility necessary.

"Jimmy talked to me about it a little bit in the offseason. Something [they] might go to a little bit and try," Tavares said. "I've been there at different instances in my career in different situations. Obviously, most of my time here has been down the middle, my natural position. But obviously, with the type of depth we have, players we have, skill sets, just do everything I can to go out there, play well, help the team, and make my teammates around me better and execute and do what I do well."

Tavares does not view the move as something that inherently improves his individual game. He framed it as an opportunity to be effective in a different role and to play off his linemates. It certainly doesn't seem like a position he prefers. But he's the ultimate team player and wouldn't indicate otherwise.

"I don't know if I look at as helping my game," he said. "I think naturally, I just want to go out there and be really effective and help the team. Sometimes you're just put in different spots, at times you're just on the wall more, less to the middle of the ice. So just understanding where you are throughout the structure and positionally, and then how I can use my skill sets through that, and create opportunities, be strong defensively."

Hiller does not anticipate a difficult transition. He noted that Tavares has played wing in Toronto at different points and that the recent World Championship experience left him comfortable in either spot. With Paul also capable of shifting, the two are expected to rotate responsibilities, including faceoffs, rather than lock into fixed positions.

"He'll take faceoffs. It'll kind of be just the two of them, kind of just rotating positions more than anything."

Hiller sees value in having two legitimate centres on the same line. It gives the coaching staff more comfort in the defensive zone, where one of the two is likely to play lower. He is also eager to see the combination produce offensively.

"I'm really looking forward to the line with John there," Hiller said. "I think it should be a dangerous O-zone heavy line. That's what I'm really looking to see from those three."

Beyond the positional fit, Hiller highlighted the presence Tavares brings. He pointed to the team's need for more offensive-zone time, better puck battles, and net-front work, areas where Tavares has long excelled.

"He's just another veteran player with a real presence," Hiller said. "And I think you know one of the things that we maybe struggled with is O-zone time, pucks, winning puck battles, getting the net front. That's what John does extremely well. So I think you know just having that in the room, knowing that's what he gives us on the ice too."

Tavares was out with a lower-body injury and while Hiller never indicated what the injury was, the former Leafs captain told Hiller he has dealt with a similar injury previously, which helped him manage the recovery timeline. Hiller said he stayed on schedule, worked hard, and never fell behind. For Tavares, the return to full practice in a regular jersey carried its own significance.

Whether the line with Paul produces the offensive-zone pressure Hiller is seeking remains to be seen once Tavares is back in games. What is clear from both the player and the coach is that the Leafs view him as ready to contribute immediately, and they are comfortable deploying him on the wing to make it happen. With a top-six group looking for a boost, Tavares' return should help in the department.

Why Maple Leafs Forward John Tavares Is Slated To Return As Winger Instead Of A Centre In Potential Return Against Canadiens

John Tavares is expected to return to the Toronto Maple Leafs lineup after roughly three weeks out, and the early indication is that he will do so on the wing.

Tavares skated on the second line of the wing alongside Nick Paul and William Nylander. He said the process has been challenging but positive, giving credit to the medical and performance staff for getting him back.

"Feeling good," Tavares said. "It's been a good three weeks now, I guess. And excited to be right there. ... Just excited, love to compete, and want to help the team."

The shift to the wing is not entirely new territory. Tavares played left wing next to Paul in the final game before he was injured. Coach Jim Hiller had spoken with him about the possibility in the offseason, and Tavares had already logged time on the wing for Canada at the World Championship in May. While he has spent the bulk of his Leafs career down the middle, he said the team's depth makes flexibility necessary.

"Jimmy talked to me about it a little bit in the offseason. Something [they] might go to a little bit and try," Tavares said. "I think helps a little bit. Different style of game, different ice surface, but obviously, that's where I played throughout May at the World Championships. So, I've been there at different instances in my career in different situations. Obviously, most of my time here has been down the middle, my natural position. But obviously, with the type of depth we have, players we have, skill sets, just do everything I can to go out there, play well, help the team, and make my teammates around me better and execute and do what I do well."

Tavares does not view the move as something that inherently improves his individual game. He framed it as an opportunity to be effective in a different role and to play off his linemates. It certainly doesn't seem like a position he prefers. But he's the ultimate team player and wouldn't indicate otherwise.

"I don't know if I look at as helping my game," he said. "I think naturally, I just want to go out there and be really effective and help the team. Sometimes you're just put in different spots, at times you're just on the wall more, less to the middle of the ice. So just understanding where you are throughout the structure and positionally, and then how I can use my skill sets through that, and create opportunities, be strong defensively."

Hiller does not anticipate a difficult transition. He noted that Tavares has played wing in Toronto at different points and that the recent World Championship experience left him comfortable in either spot. With Paul also capable of shifting, the two are expected to rotate responsibilities, including faceoffs, rather than lock into fixed positions.

"He'll take faceoffs. It'll kind of be just the two of them, kind of just rotating positions more than anything."

Hiller sees value in having two legitimate centres on the same line. It gives the coaching staff more comfort in the defensive zone, where one of the two is likely to play lower. He is also eager to see the combination produce offensively.

"I'm really looking forward to the line with John there," Hiller said. "I think it should be a dangerous O-zone heavy line. That's what I'm really looking to see from those three."

Beyond the positional fit, Hiller highlighted the presence Tavares brings. He pointed to the team's need for more offensive-zone time, better puck battles, and net-front work, areas where Tavares has long excelled.

"He's just another veteran player with a real presence," Hiller said. "And I think you know one of the things that we maybe struggled with is O-zone time, pucks, winning puck battles, getting the net front. That's what John does extremely well. So I think you know just having that in the room, knowing that's what he gives us on the ice too."

Tavares was out with a lower-body injury and while Hiller never indicated what the injury was, the former Leafs captain told Hiller he has dealt with a similar injury previously, which helped him manage the recovery timeline. Hiller said he stayed on schedule, worked hard, and never fell behind. For Tavares, the return to full practice in a regular jersey carried its own significance.

Whether the line with Paul produces the offensive-zone pressure Hiller is seeking remains to be seen once Tavares is back in games. What is clear from both the player and the coach is that the Leafs view him as ready to contribute immediately, and they are comfortable deploying him on the wing to make it happen. With a top-six group looking for a boost, Tavares' return should help in the department.

#2936. Lions have new rallying cry after Jack Campbell’s terrifying injury

The Lions have a new mantra as they prepare to play without injured linebacker Jack Campbell: "Play like 46."

The message has become a centerpiece throughout the organization following the worrisome neck injury Campbell suffered during Sunday's 19-15 victory over the Vikings.

Campbell, 26, was carted off the field after attempting to make a tackle on standout Vikings tight end T.J. Hockenson during the second half.

Following the collision, Campbell remained motionless on the turf before being transported to a hospital.

Jack Campbell is tended to by trainers after suffering a neck injury during the Lions' Week 8 win over the Packers on Nov. 1, 2026, at Ford Field in Detroit. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Defensive coordinator Kelvin Sheppard said Thursday the team adopted its new motto, "Play like 46," following an emotional meeting Wednesday.

"So, the theme has been let's prepare and play like 46," Sheppard told reporters. "Every day I wake up, 'What would 46 want me to do today? Would he want us to sulk and feel sorry?' I can guarantee y'all know Jack. Absolutely not.

"He'll be pissed off if he found out guys are crying and guys are like, about him," he added. That's just his makeup and that's what makes him the person that he is. So, that's what we're doing. And everything we do, every meeting I told the unit, every walkthrough, every play, you all know how Jack prepares. You all know how he plays. The way you honor him is to wipe those tears from your eyes and make sure we prepare and play like him. So, that's the theme moving forward to this week."

The Lions received encouraging news Wednesday when Campbell released a statement regarding his recovery.

"I'm incredibly encouraged by the progress I've made in such a short period of time," he said, via the Lions. "I have full movement in my lower body, and I'm continuing to make progress with movement in my upper body. I know there's still work ahead, but I remain very optimistic. I'm taking things one day at a time, staying positive, and focusing on getting better."

Jack Campbell is carted off the field after suffering a neck injury during the Lions' Week 8 win over the Packers. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Campbell remains hospitalized, with no timetable announced for his return to football activities.

The 26-year-old was the 18th overall pick in the 2023 NFL Draft and has been a starter since his rookie campaign.

#2935. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2934. Raptors secure first NBA Cup win vs Bucks

The Toronto Raptors secured their first NBA Cup victory of the season, defeating the Milwaukee Bucks 121-107 at Fiserv Forum.

Raptors guard Alijah Martin was the standout performer, recording 27 points off the bench and providing the spark Toronto needed to secure the win.

The Raptors were without Scottie Barnes and Immanuel Quickley, who were both sidelined with injuries. In their absence, Jamal Shead and Collin Murray-Boyles were called upon to step up, joining Ja'Kobe Walter, RJ Barrett and Jakob Poeltl in the starting line-up.

The Bucks got off to a strong start, with Jaime Jaquez Jr. opening the scoring with a lay-up and a three-pointer. Milwaukee also found success on the offensive glass, building an early 7-0 run before Murray-Boyles ended it with a dunk off a nice pass from Shead.

Toronto struggled to find its rhythm offensively, continuing a trend seen throughout much of the season. Despite creating quality scoring opportunities, the Raptors had difficulty converting their looks, while Milwaukee continued to capitalize on second-chance opportunities.

The Bucks extended their advantage with a 10-0 run following a Raptors timeout midway through the opening quarter. However, Toronto responded late in the frame, with Murray-Boyles, Martin and Jamison Battle helping spark a 17-5 run to cut the deficit to just two points.

Martin was particularly impressive, knocking down two three-pointers and converting both of his free throws. He finished the opening quarter with a career-high 14 points, showcasing his scoring ability from beyond the arc.

A subsequent steal led to a Walter dunk, bringing the Raptors within five. Shead continued to find Poeltl, setting him up for two more baskets as Toronto pulled within one.

Martin continued to deliver from beyond the arc, knocking down another big three to bring his total to 27 points.

The two teams continued to trade baskets, with Milwaukee answering nearly every Toronto scoring run. The Bucks refused to go away quietly, keeping the game competitive as both sides looked to gain control down the stretch.

#2933. Trump says U.S. carried out ‘minor strikes’ on Iran as missiles hit Qeshm Island

President Donald Trump told reporters Friday that the U.S. carried out "minor strikes" on Iran, amid reports that two missiles struck Qeshm Island near the Strait of Hormuz.

The missiles struck the island on Friday, Iranian news agency Tasnim reported, according to Reuters. Tasnim said there was no official information on damage, Reuters added.

The island lies off Iran's southern coast, near the Strait of Hormuz, the vital artery for oil and gas that has become a flashpoint in the war.

Speaking to reporters Friday evening after landing in West Palm Beach, Florida, Trump didn't comment specifically on Qeshm Island, saying: "I don't want to talk about it."

But he added: "We do minor strikes, but they knock out. They built a little bit of an anti-aircraft device, and we knocked it out in about 15 minutes, maybe five." He did not explicitly confirm that he was referring to the reported strikes on Qeshm.

The strikes are the first since Tuesday's midterm elections, after analysts and regional officials had debated whether the vote would lead Trump toward further escalation or renewed diplomacy.

The U.S. struck the same island Monday, in what a U.S. official told MS Now was a "defensive measure."

Trump also told reporters Friday that Iran wants "to make a deal very badly. They have 300% inflation. Their navy's gone. Their airport, everything's gone, and they want to make a deal."

The International Monetary Fund forecast that Iran's consumer prices would rise by an average of 78.6% in 2026, in a report published Oct. 13.

When asked if he wanted to make a deal "really soon," Trump said: "Well, we have nobody getting hurt, nobody getting killed.

"You know, this is a war that we may have been there for seven months, but we probably fought for three or four weeks, really fought. And again, you look at World War One; that's a war. World War Two; that's a war."

Iran has said it wants more than "paper commitments" from the U.S. before entering a new phase of diplomatic talks.

"We must see practical measures by the Americans," Ali Bagheri Kani, deputy secretary of Iran's Supreme National Security Council, told state broadcaster IRIB on Friday, according to Press TV.

#2926. Steelers Legend Sends Stern Message To Mike McCarthy Amid HC's Praise for 6-2 Start

The Pittsburgh Steelers are sitting atop their division with a 6-2 record after struggling at the start of the season. But for Steelers icon James Harrison, it is pertinent that the Steelers don't get too comfortable. For head coach Mike McCarthy, 6-2 is not the standard Pittsburgh should be aiming.

"Yes, he deserves the credit. He has done well so far," Two-time Super Bowl champion James Harrison said on his podcast Deebo & Joe. "And I hate to sound cynical about a 6-2 start, but the reality of it for me is that the standard in Pittsburgh can't just be winning regular-season games. Like, we already did that under Mike Tomlin, making the playoffs and, you know, getting bounced out."

Mike McCarthy took over a team that had one head coach for 19 seasons. In all those years, Mike Tomlin sure pulled winning seasons. But he was able to win just one Super Bowl, despite making the postseason 13 times. In the past three seasons, the Steelers were knocked out of the Wild Card game itself. Harrison alluded that McCarthy's real test is whether he is able to get over that hump.

"The goal wasn't to go from winning season to winning season, bro. The goal is to go from winning season to winning championships. Anything that isn't advancing into the playoffs and winning minimum, if we do that, it justifies the change that we made. But the thing that's going to be winning-winning is holding that Lombardi trophy at the end of the season," Harrison further added.

Harrison further said that he is going to "hold" his judgment as he wants to see how well the team does in the playoffs.

While Harrison asks more of the Steelers, they have made huge progress themselves. Though the team was sitting at 2-2 after the first four games, McCarthy and his team switched gears, winning four consecutive games.

Their latest game saw the Steelers win a division rivalry against the Cleveland Browns 27-6, snatching the top spot in the AFC North.

"Definitely, you've got to say 'trending' winning four in a row," McCarthy said. "I think we're exactly what our record says we are. We've really focused on improving in certain areas. We're doing that. Our run defense has really set the bar really high these last two weeks. I just thought the way they started the game was really impressive."

However, the remainder of the season still awaits. Pittsburgh has matchups remaining with the Cincinnati Bengals, Denver Broncos, and the Jacksonville Jaguars, all worthy opponents. The last two are also 6-2 currently, which means they will be a tough fight for Pittsburgh.

However, the current run of the team sure looks promising. Entering Week 7, the Steelers had less than a 50% chance of making the playoffs, according to The Athletic's playoff simulator. However, now 6-2, their odds have bumped up to a hopeful 78%.

For now, McCarthy is focusing on the positives and negatives of the team, and highlighted Jaylen Warren and Rico Dowdle's contributions.

"It starts up front, obviously," said McCarthy. "We've got two dynamic backs. Jaylen is on fire. You can't get him the ball enough. Rico, he's playing well, too. I think those two are hitting their stride. That's important for those two men to touch the ball each and every week."

Warren ranks 1st among 45 qualified running backs with a PFF rushing grade of 91.1, while Dowdle played his part, logging 56 yards against the Browns.

The Steelers now have a bye in Week 9 and will have much-needed rest before they face division rival Cincinnati in Week 10.

#2923. Eagles locker room rallying around rocket-armed QB Joe Milton

Eagles locker room rallying around rocket-armed QB Joe Milton  originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

If you happened to be in South Philadelphia on Wednesday afternoon and have supersonic hearing, you might have already known the Eagles have a new starting quarterback.

"He zips it. He zips it," receiver Darius Cooper said shaking his head. "You can hear it flying in the air."

The rest of the world found out the Eagles made the decision to bench Tanner McKee and go with Milton from ESPN less than 2 hours after head coach Nick Sirianni failed mention a thing during his Wednesday press conference. And neither Milton nor McKee were available to reporters after Wednesday's practice.

But despite some janky attempts at obfuscation from a couple of starting offensive linemen on Wednesday, the team knows who is starting against the Giants on Sunday afternoon. It's Milton.

Dallas Goedert said he and his teammates found out the news in a meeting minutes before the public heard.

"We were kind of hoping that it would stay under wraps," Goedert said. "It would be a competitive advantage and they wouldn't find out until Saturday. But it didn't take long for people to know."

Milton, 26, was as sixth-round pick out of Tennessee back in 2024 and has played in games with the Patriots and Cowboys, but this will be his first start. The Eagles just signed him off the Cowboys' practice squad on Oct. 16. It took him a week to get bumped up to the backup job and three weeks to become the starter.

After Jalen Hurts was lost for the season with blood clots in his lungs, the Eagles gave McKee three starts and the offense has been struggling. So now they're turning to Milton and hoping he provides a spark. We'll see if Milton can do that on Sunday, but he did seem to inject some juice into the locker room to kick off this week.

"He's a real good athlete. That stands out," Goedert said. "He's got a cannon of an arm. Today it seemed like he was really accurate, got through his reads good, made check downs when he needed. But it'll be exciting for him to go out there. I know he's excited to get on the field and play. Going to do whatever I can to help him succeed."

Milton is 6-foot-5, 236 pounds and he is elusive in the pocket. That elusiveness will help an offense that has been giving up too many pressures, especially against the blitz, all season.

But the thing everyone always brings up about Milton is his rocket of a right arm. That was no different after Wednesday's practice.

"He can spin it," tight end Eli Stowers said. "You feel it when he throws it to you and it hits your hands pretty quick. When he lets it go down the field, it's pretty good."

"It's kind of perfect for a pass catcher," Goedert said. "The balls that are in the air for a long time when you can think, those ones are the scariest ones. The ones that get on you and you just have to catch it, those are the easy ones. It seems like he's been putting the ball in a good spot and I'm excited to see what he does Sunday."

Goedert on Wednesday was surprised to find out that Sunday's game will be Milton's first-career NFL start. Goedert said he thought he read that Milton had already started six or seven games; that's not the case. Milton has been in the NFL for two seasons and he's played in five games and has thrown 53 passes — but he'll make his first start in a few days.

Despite that inexperience, Milton apparently doesn't lack for confidence. Goedert didn't even seem to question the thought that Milton had started games before.

"He's got a lot of swag to him. He's confident," veteran receiver Hollywood Brown said. "He's excited for this opportunity as well. That was his whole reason to come here, to get a chance like this. He's excited for it.

"It's a testament to the journey he's been on. He's probably been that confident his whole life. So for him, it seems natural. For all of us, we're rallying around it, trying to get a W."

Even though Milton has shown confidence, the Eagles are going to try to help him as much as possible on Sunday. Cooper said he wants to help give Milton tips on where their motions are coming from, Goedert said he wants to be a reliable outlet, Brown said he wants to be able to create after the catch. The Eagles are likely without DeVonta Smith and Dontayvion Wicks again, so any little thing will help.

Milton didn't sign with the Eagles until Oct. 16, but he immediately fit in. Stowers said that Milton has been "a light in the locker room" since his arrival and praised the quarterback for making an attempt to get to know all of his new teammates on a personal level. That goes a long way, especially now that Milton will lead them into a game on Sunday.

"I'm excited for his opportunity," Cooper said.

#2922. Ralf Schumacher slams Colton Herta's F1 prospects: "He's just burning fuel"

On the face of it, Friday at Interlagos could hardly have gone any better for Colton Herta. The Cadillac test driver replaced Sergio Perez in first practice for the 2026 Sao Paulo Grand Prix, completed 24 laps and secured the final point he needed for his FIA super licence. On paper, the American is now eligible to race in Formula 1 in 2027.

Former F1 driver turned pundit Ralf Schumacher, however, is having none of it. Asked whether Herta could be a candidate for the second Cadillac seat alongside Perez, the Sky Germany pundit replied: "Only if Easter and Christmas fall on the same day. Seriously, it's pointless. He's just burning fuel, to borrow a phrase from Franz Tost. I don't even know what the point of it is. You might as well put someone else in the car, give some young drivers a chance."

Schumacher was particularly critical of Herta's Formula 2 campaign: "He was really that bad in Formula 2. As far as I'm concerned, he hasn't earned it at all. I think it's great that they're trying to promote American drivers. But it has to make sense, and he has no place in Formula 1."

Instead of Herta, Schumacher would prefer to see young drivers given an opportunity. As an example, he points to McLaren junior Leonardo Fornaroli, who has won championships in both Formula 2 and Formula 3, describing him as "an incredibly strong driver". Otherwise, however, finding the right partner for Perez would be "very, very difficult".

Herta had moved to Formula 2 to prove himself worthy of a Formula 1 seat and accumulate super licence points. But he currently sits just 19th in the championship standings with 26 points, having failed to claim a victory or even a podium finish so far. Even Formula 2 CEO Bruno Michel admits that Herta has struggled more than expected.

The American therefore secured his super licence through an alternative route. In addition to the points he earned in IndyCar, where he won nine races and finished runner-up in the 2024 championship, he collected further points through his Friday practice appearances for Cadillac.

Racing Bulls boss 'understands frustration' over driver decision delay

One of Cadillac's 2027 seats is already confirmed: Perez extended his contract until 2029 at the end of October. Whether Valtteri Bottas will remain with the team, however, is still unclear. Several other names have been mentioned alongside Herta, with George Russell, for example, putting Mercedes talent Frederik Vesti forward as a candidate.

In their head-to-head comparison during Friday's practice session at Interlagos, Herta was around nine tenths of a second slower than Bottas.

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers handles the ball against the Portland Trail Blazers on Friday, Nov. 6, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images
